@@25275129 <p> The chances of limiting climate change appear to be growing slimmer by the day -- and this may have big implications for Australia 's wildlife . <p> Recently a number of crocodiles have been trapped in the Mary River , just 105 kilometres north of Noosa and 250km south of their usual range . <p> Irukandji jellyfish too , appear to be expanding south , with 10 suspected stings near Fraser Island and a child stung at Mooloolaba last year . <p> Numerous tropical fish have been recorded up to 1,000 kilometres south of their traditional range , such as the Great Barrier Reef 's lemon-peel angelfish which turned up on Lord Howe Island in 2009 , and habitat-modifying sea urchins have landed in Tasmania . <p> Crocodiles like this one have been showing up regularly in the Mary River , 100 kms north of Noosa . <p> ABC Wide Bay : Brad Marsellos <p> The average annual monthly maximum temperature difference between Gladstone and Noosa is 1.8 degrees Celsius , according to Bureau of Meteorology records . <p> Although a 2-degree average temperature increase does not mean all regions will warm uniformly , if we take it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would be inside the present range of crocodiles . <p> Crocodiles have been known to travel hundreds of kilometres in order to find new territory , according to Darwin zoologist Adam Britton . <p> " If two points are connected by water then they can get there . And the only thing that really stops them if there 's water involved is temperature . " <p> Although they are fairly generalist in nature , there are a few environmental factors that crocodiles prefer when seeking new habitat . <p> " They tend to prefer tidal river systems -- everything from the mangrove habitats you find in the estuaries , up into the less-saline habitat you see further upstream . " <p> Certainly estuarine habitats exist all the way down the Queensland coast and into New South Wales . <p> But there is uncertainty about whether the recent instances of crocodiles in southern waters is climate related or due to increasing numbers . <p> Crocodile populations have dramatically recovered from the brink of extinction since the 1970s , and the need for new territory may push some individuals to move outside @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Science ( DES ) spokesperson said they currently " do n't have evidence " to suggest crocodiles are expanding south . <p> " To establish a better scientific understanding of Queensland 's wild population of estuarine crocodiles , the Queensland Government commenced a three-year crocodile survey and monitoring program in April 2017 , " they said by email . <p> " We will be considering size , habitat , distribution and abundance of estuarine crocodiles in Queensland waterways . " <p> But Professor Stephen Williams from James Cook University ( JCU ) agreed that as the climate warmed , southward migration was inevitable . <p> " They 're a big reptile , they 're reliant on warmer water and sunbaking to keep warm and their southern extent certainly will be affected by the temperature . I 'm guessing that 'll continue and move further south , " he said . <p> " I was at the beach just up at the Daintree the other day and we saw a three-metre croc swim past just out past the surf line ... it 's like yeah that 'll be good fun @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Although " Irukandji syndrome " was known from the 1940s in North Queensland , its cause was unknown until toxinologist Jack Barnes allowed himself , his nine-year-old son , and a volunteer lifesaver to be stung by a jellyfish the size of a fingernail . <p> All were hospitalised but recovered , and Barnes documented the sting symptoms of what came to be known as the Irukandji jellyfish . <p> Several species of Irukandji are common in Australia 's tropical waters but numerous stings have recently occurred around Fraser Island , and one as far south as Mooloolaba on the Sunshine Coast last year . <p> Despite there being limited knowledge of Irukandji biology , toxinologist associate professor Jamie Seymour from JCU who has studied them extensively , said they were already responding to warming conditions . <p> " If you go back 50 years ago , you find that the stinger season was around two months long . Now the season is about six to seven months long , " he said . <p> " We looked at how far south the stings were 50 years ago and they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ where we 're getting stings now , which is the southern end of Fraser Island . " <p> Jack Barnes stung himself and his nine-year-old son with an Irukandji . <p> Jack Barnes stung himself and his nine-year-old son with an Irukandji . <p> Supplied : Lisa-Ann Gershwin <p> Although some have argued Irukandji live in the reef and so are limited in how far south they can travel , Dr Seymour does not think that is the case . <p> Juveniles inhabit water that is less saline than open ocean , but that may be shallow inshore waters or even around estuarine systems . <p> " I think the major reason people think you only find them around reefs is because that 's where people swim , " he said . <p> " By and large , the East Australia Current is going to push them further south until it gets too cold and they ca n't survive , so their southern distribution is temperature limited -- we 're fairly certain about that . " <p> CEO of the Queensland Tourism Industry Council Daniel Gschwind said the tourism industry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ adjust their advice to tourists accordingly . <p> " If something changes in our environment then we have to make sure people are aware of this , " he said . <p> But he also cautioned it was important to keep things in perspective . <p> " Do you know what is the greatest risk to tourists that come to Australia ? Car travel followed by beach safety , " he said . <p> Dr Seymour also said the threat posed by Irukandji was sometimes overblown . <p> " The numbers are fuzzy but we reckon we get something like 400 or 500 stings per year in Queensland , and out of the entire history that we know of we 've only had two deaths worldwide . " <h> Nowhere to go but up <p> White lemuroid possums are one of many species that may run out of mountains to climb . <p> White lemuroid possums are one of many species that may run out of mountains to climb . <p> www.wettropics.gov.au <p> While marine animals are able to migrate north and south in search of cooler waters , a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ same option . <p> As temperatures rise , species like the white lemuroid ringtail possum in the wet tropics of North Queensland are being pushed to higher , colder altitudes . <p> The problem is , with Australia 's relatively low mountain ranges , there is only so high they can go , according to Professor Stephen Williams . <p> " Fifteen years ago , I was happily going along studying ecology and rainforest biodiversity , and then I did some work on climate change as a side project and realised that almost everything I worked on could go extinct this century , " he said . <p> " The mountains here are n't very big , they only go up to 1,500 metres . Most of them are actually only 1,000 to 1,200 metres , so they just slowly contract up the mountain until there 's nowhere for them to go . " <p> For Australia 's mountain-dwelling animals , Professor Williams said the 2-degree target was critical . <p> " Our models predict that we do n't really get any extinction until about one-and-a-half degrees , and that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ survived one-and-a-half degrees in the past , " he said . <p> " The extinctions do n't really start to kick in until about 2 degrees , and then they start to accelerate rapidly beyond 2 degrees . @@25298163 <h> Letters : Energy policy , wage rises and Hayne commission <h> We 'll never have Paris <p> Laura Tingle wrongly claims the National Energy Guarantee scheme " focuses not just on emissions but also on the increasingly more politically important ( and sellable ) issues of reliability and price " ( " The government 's persuaders switch from tax to energy " , June 23 ) . The key object of NEG is to reduce emissions and meet the Paris target . <p> This reflects decisions by Malcolm Turnbull and Energy Minister Josh Frydenberg taken on advice from supposed experts on the Energy Security Board and the head of Mr Turnbull 's department . This is now to be reflected in a socialist regulatory body described as providing a guaranteed method of supplying energy . That will not only entrench high prices but add to them as coal-fired generators are replaced by more expensive renewable and gas . <p> Such a policy is dated . The Liberals in Monash Forum have now shown that a large number of coal-fired power stations are under construction or being planned in other countries ; the EU body CAN Europe has acknowledged @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , not a single EU state is reducing their greenhouse gas emissions at a rate sufficient to meet those targets ; and the US has withdrawn from that agreement . <p> In effect the targets have been moved many years out in time . The Turnbull government should follow suit and adopt the more sensible schemes being increasingly used overseas , viz in practice move Paris and provide scope for increased coal-fired productions . <h> Related Quotes <p> New Zealand has suffered the same challenge as Australia , that is , a failure by the major lenders to share their repayment history information ( RHI ) . This reluctance has seen the NZ system fail to deliver for consumers . This is why the Australian government 's initiative to mandate the reporting of RHI by the major banks in Australia is so important . <p> The comments in regard to the value of RHI similarly miss the point . The inclusion of other data elements in a credit report , while welcome , fall well short of global best practice and mean that lenders will not be able to identify @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of credit . In doing so it means that lenders will not be on notice that any additional credit product may not be suitable for the borrower . This has the potential to create more instances of hardship for Australians - the issue the CALC 's Gerard Brody says his organisation is concerned to prevent . <p> In addition to putting more Australians at risk of financial hardship by taking on more credit than they can manage , an issue brought into sharp focus in the recent royal commission , the delay in reporting the repayment data also has the undesirable effect of causing creditworthy consumers to pay more for their loans . This is because without RHI , smaller lenders are at a competitive disadvantage and are impeded in providing the better deals that an improved credit reporting system would deliver to consumers . <p> Whether someone is a good credit risk or already overcommitted , a continued delay in the implementation of the government 's legislation to mandate comprehensive credit reporting is a lose-lose proposition for Australian consumers . <p> Simon Bligh CEO <p> illion <p> ? @@25315210 <h> Are shipping emissions adding to weather events in China ? <h> Are shipping emissions adding to weather events in China ? <h> IMPACT Marine diesel emissions have a high concentration of sulphur dioxide . Photo : Shutterstock <p> In the 1960s , Massachusetts Institute of Technology mathematician and meteorologist Edward Lorenz constructed a model of the weather that showed how a butterfly flapping its wings in South America could potentially affect weather in New York 's Central Park . <p> Is it so outlandish , then , in line with his ' chaos theory ' , to think a ship in the Indian Ocean could affect the weather in China ? <p> If , as it is widely assumed , higher pollution levels are linked to more severe weather patterns , it stands to reason the higher the emissions , the more extreme the weather . <p> This theory was backed by a recent study led by Pennsylvania State University professor of atmospheric science , Michael Mann . <p> " We came as close as one can to demonstrating a direct link between climate change and a large family of extreme recent weather events , " Professor Mann @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the world 's leading source of air pollution : <p> * Heavy snow hitting Shanghai for the first time in decades . <p> * Torrential rain and flooding . <p> * Gale-force winds . <p> And that 's just in the past few months . <p> China knows it has a pollution problem and has set stringent guidelines , such as barring the opening of new coal plants and requiring existing plants to cut emissions , restricting the number of cars in major urban areas , and even removing coal boilers from homes . <p> But while China reports some improvement in air quality , there 's only so much the country can do to rein in the weather , as well as the causes of that weather . <p> A significant source of pollution is outside the country 's direct control . There 's evidence that extreme weather is linked to pollution generated from the shipping industry . <p> A paper published in the scientific journal , Geophysical Research Letters , suggests areas marked by popular shipping lanes in the Indian Ocean and South China Sea experience twice as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Here 's how it happens : <p> Ships burning marine diesel emit exhaust high in sulphur dioxide , which results in the formation of sulphate aerosols in the atmosphere . These aerosols act as nuclei around which water vapour condenses , creating clouds . <p> Typically , marine clouds comprise larger droplets and do not reach higher altitudes . However , these aerosol-formed clouds consist of smaller particles , which are more easily carried upwards by convection , resulting in the increase in lightning . <p> So how does this relate to weather in China ? <p> Researchers such as atmospheric scientist Daniel Rosenfeld , of Israel 's Hebrew University , believe these ship-generated aerosols can impact on weather systems in a number of ways , such as transferring precipitation ( and pollution ) from one area to another , potentially thousands of kilometres away . <p> In other words , ships in the Indian Ocean could be impacting on the weather in Beijing . <p> The aerosol cloud-seeding creates droplets so small they no longer band together to form rain . <p> Say goodbye , then , to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pollution and rid urban areas of the build up of smog . <p> Less rain , combined with stagnation of air , leads to poor air quality for China -- no matter how far it cuts its own emissions from factories and cars . <p> " The haze-inducing conditions are 50 per cent more likely to form between 2050 and 2099 than they were from 1950 to 1999 , " says climate change writer Robinson Meyer in the The Atlantic . <p> " And once those patterns fall into place in any one episode , they are 80 per cent more likely to persist . " <p> Researchers James J. Winebrake , professor at Rochester ( New York ) Institute of Technology , and James J. Corbett , professor at the University of Delaware , say that since 2008 , ship pollution exposure has contributed to more than 1.5 million premature deaths worldwide . <p> Other studies have found that emissions from shipping are responsible for 24,000 premature deaths annually in East Asia , with 75 per cent of those deaths occurring in China . <p> It 's important to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and economic damage from pollution-generated flooding , hurricanes , and other extreme weather events . <p> The link is clear -- a reduction in sulphur emissions from ships leads to a reduction in sulphate aerosols , which in turn cuts down not only on the direct ill effects of shipping pollution , but on the potential for catastrophic weather events as well .

" <p> MFRC members met with WA government representatives in Perth on June 19 , the council 's first gathering . <p> Among the MFRC 's priorities are the implementation of a Martuwarra Fitzroy River Catchment Management Plan and a freeze on all future water allocations in the river catchment until a plan is in place . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ declaration handed down by Traditional Owners in 2016 to protect the river . <p> The council was set up following a two-day meeting in Fitzroy Crossing in May . <p> Native title has been determined along the length of the Fitzroy River and across its catchment . Traditional owners also own about half of the pastoral leases in the catchment . <p> It is the first time Traditional Owners hold native title rights across an entire catchment area and it 's seen as a precedent for how government and industry engage with Indigenous locals . <p> There is currently no management and protection plan for the river , the group said . <p> A WA government spokesperson said it had begun comprehensive community consultation across the east Kimberley as part of a commitment towards creating Fitzroy River National Park to ensure long-term health of the river and sustainable economic development . <p> A community workshop was held in Fitzroy Crossing in March with about 80 key stakeholders , including Traditional Owners and representatives from Aboriginal groups , pastoralists , environmental organisations , government agencies , industry and the wider community @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continuing and was expected to take 18 months . <p> " As part of the McGowan Government 's election commitment to create a Fitzroy Management Plan , the Department is working on a water allocation plan , " the spokesperson said . <p> " This plan will determine how much water can be taken from the system , while taking into account the environmental , social and cultural needs of the area . "

@@25358850 <p> Walrus use sea ice to rest while hunting . But at 2C the Arctic is " very likely " to be ice-free one year in ten , scientists have found ( Photo : Brad Benter/USFWS <p> A leaked draft of a major UN climate change report shows growing certainty that 2C , once shorthand for a ' safe ' amount of planetary warming , would be a dangerous step for humanity . <p> The authors make clear the difference between warming of 1.5C and 2C would be " substantial " and damaging to communities , economies and ecosystems across the world . <p> In 2015 , the Paris Agreement established twin goals to hold temperature rise from pre-industrial times " well below 2C " and strive for 1.5C . <p> The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) has since been working to assess the difference between those targets , with a view to publishing a sweeping analysis of all available research in October this year . <p> CHN has compared the January and June drafts . The new version builds a stronger case for governments to rapidly cut carbon pollution . It also strikes a marginally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In January , authors said every 0.5C added to today 's level of 1C of warming would " increase " the risks of various impacts . That wording has been beefed up throughout the new summary , which now predicts " substantial increases " in those risks . <p> Bill Hare , a physicist and CEO of Climate Analytics , said the new draft had made big steps forward in clarifying the difference between the two Paris temperature goals . <p> " If one looks across many parts of the report there are really very substantial improvements since January , " he said . <p> Since the first draft was circulated , a number of studies focusing on the differing impacts of 1.5C and 2C warming have been published . New evidence on the impacts on species , the economy , physical systems and invasive pests has been incorporated in the report . <p> A report released in April allowed the IPCC to predict that at 2C it was " very likely that there will be at least one sea ice free Arctic summer per decade " , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The draft was also changed in line with new research on economic impacts , finding " growth is projected to be lower at 2C than at 1.5C of global warming for many developed and developing countries " . <p> The major outstanding question about the 1.5C target is : is it feasible ? In the new draft , the scientists write " there is no simple answer " . <p> On current levels of pollution , the world is warming roughly 0.2C each decade . If that continues , the 1.5C threshold will be crossed in the 2040s , the report says . <p> However comparison of the drafts reveals a significant increase in the " carbon budget " -- the total mass of greenhouse gases that can be emitted before the world will be committed to warming past 1.5C . <p> The January draft found a maximum of 580 gigatonnes of CO2 would give a 50% chance of limiting warming to 1.5C . In the new draft , that number has been increased by a third to 750GtCO2 . <p> In January , authors wrote scenarios that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ already out of reach " . That language has been dropped , although the new draft does not say these higher probabilities are now considered feasible . <p> A coal power plant in Tianjin , China . Primary energy from coal must be reduced by two thirds by 2030 for 1.5C to be possible , the draft says ( Photo : Shubert Ciencia ) <p> CHN asked IPCC authors and scientists not directly involved in the summary to help explain the difference between the budgets . None were willing to comment on the record . <p> One researcher said the process for calculating the budget was " highly intransparent " . <p> The calculation of carbon budgets relies on assumptions and different approaches produce numbers that vary by " more than 50% " , according to the new draft . That covers a real world set of emissions cuts that range from virtually unachievable to challenging but possible . <p> The first draft of the special report was released for feedback from researchers in January . After receiving tens of thousands of comments it was revised and on 4 June @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The final government draft summary published on this website contains detailed annotations by CHN reporters on many of the changes made between the first and second drafts of the report . <p> Both versions of the report find the 1.5C goal requires CO2 emissions from electricity to reach net zero by mid-century . <p> The second draft adds a note that : " The political , economic , social and technical feasibility of solar energy , wind energy and electricity storage technologies increased over the past few years , signalling that such a system transition in electricity generation may be underway . " <p> The latest draft summary places more emphasis on efforts to cut emissions before 2030 . This mirrors many countries ' pledges to the Paris Agreement , which set emissions targets for that year . <p> Pressure is growing for these promises to be increased . On Monday , 14 EU countries called on the European Commission 's long term climate strategy , which is under development , to align with the 1.5C limit . <p> The summary elaborates on what a " rapid and far-reaching @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deployment needs to accelerate further for 1.5C to be possible , the draft says , with primary energy from coal falling two thirds by 2030 . For comparison , the International Energy Agency forecasts coal use increasing slightly over the period , based on existing and signposted policies . <p> It calls for sustainable management of competing demands on the land . This includes " diet changes " -- code for the rich eating less steak -- and " sustainable intensification " of farming , which is viewed with suspicion by many environmentalists . <p> Radical emissions cuts are also needed in industry , transport and buildings , where it says technology exists but faces economic and social barriers . <p> The final section deals with sustainable development and how efforts to meet the 1.5C limit interact with goals like eradicating poverty . <p> In the first summary , the authors warned there was a " high chance " the 1.5C target " might not be feasible " because efforts to remove carbon from the atmosphere , through tree-planting or use of carbon capture with biofuels , can conflict with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ production . <p> This language has been toned down , instead concluding the feasibility of such methods " depends on scale , and the implications for land , water and energy use " . <p> We 're still softening the language and placating our useless politicians , pretending that we do n't have to move far and fast . <p> It 's confounding , because this conciliatory approach has given us 20 years of delay . <p> john <p> And around the fringes there will be some progress but not substantial . @@25370227 <p> Analysis for The Conversation by Christine Jubb , Professor of Accounting , Swinburne University of Technology <p> The implications of climate change risks for corporate stakeholders are often poorly understood . Possibly least understood within this group is the role of those with financial expertise . We investigated and have produced a working paper on factors that influence accountants ' involvement in managing climate change risk in Australia . <p> Companies have been under greater pressure to disclose their exposure to risks of climate change since the 2014 G20 meeting in Australia . This created the Taskforce on Climate Change-Related Financial Risk Disclosure ( TCFD . Its recommended disclosures were issued in June 2017 . <p> In a speech this month , Australian Securities and Investments Commissioner John Price reinforced these recommendations . He made clear climate risk is an ASIC priority . Directors who fail to properly consider and disclose climate risks could face lawsuits , he warned . <h> So where do accountants come into this ? <p> It can be argued that those with financial expertise , such as accountants , have the tools to provide crucial information to management on potential risks embedded in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ limited role in assessing climate change risk . However , our comparative study reveals shifts in climate management towards those with financial expertise . <p> ASIC commissioner John Price 's warning that directors must consider climate risks is a pointer to accountants ' role in quantifying those risks.Lisa Creffield/Youtube <p> A principal conclusion from our research is that , together with engineers and other technical experts on climate change , those with financial expertise are significantly more intertwined in assessing and mitigating the risk than before . <p> The study involved semi-structured interviews with managers directly involved in emissions management for some of the largest Australian companies . These took place before and after the 2014 repeal of the carbon tax . In 2013 , we interviewed 39 managers across 18 companies . In 2016 it was 14 managers and 11 companies . <p> The key finding from the 2013 interviews was that engineers and environmental specialists dominated emissions management . Those with financial expertise had minimal involvement . Many interviewees claimed that , because of the complexity and technicalities , only professionals with engineering or environmental science backgrounds had @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , one financial professional leading a team in the field asserted that accountants , as risk management experts , could bring significant value to their companies : <p> I think that accountants have a lot of credibility ... because when it comes to emissions ... I think I can put forward the business case of why it 's important ... I think , that means ... it 's better received within the company than if I was ... an engineer or an environmental scientist . <p> Both financial and non-financial experts shared this view . One sustainability professional explained how a limited financial understanding leads to an inability to appropriately use techniques common in finance , such as target setting and performance evaluation . <p> Well , I think if you had the accounting knowledge ... it the target would be far more accurate , and probably a lot higher than what we 've set . <p> With our commitment to the Paris climate agreement , one might have expected companies to more urgently reduce emissions . In general , though , the second round of interviews reveals that companies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has significantly diminished . <h> Financial expertise now coming to the fore <p> However , the involvement of those with financial expertise in climate change risk management has increased . Many viewed this as positive and potentially useful for boardrooms . <p> Whether it was the carbon tax that brought finance team attention , or organisational learning , more recent interviews found evidence of greater acceptance of climate change as a financial ( and other ) material risk . The ASIC commissioner 's speech advocating TCFD-type disclosures suggests the issue is not merely one of eco-efficiency , but one of commercial substance of relevance to company directors . <p> From the interview data , one possible explanation for increased collaboration between technical and financial expertise is greater acceptance of climate change issues as material risks to companies : <p> Management of carbon is fundamentally a risk-management exercise ... It is a material risk ... if we do n't think about the long-term risks ... and what are the strategies that we need to mitigate ... <p> The best way to present ... climate-related information to ... management ... is in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , commercial risk , strategic risk ... it 's all risk . <p> Barriers to collaboration between technical and financial experts still exist . These include geographic co-location and some accountants being unable to step outside traditional roles . <p> With regulators ' increased interest in measurement and disclosure of climate change risk , the landscape is changing again . We anticipate better integration of the assessment and mitigation of climate change risk with strengthened expertise being brought to bear . This includes greater involvement from the technical expert on the ground through to the boardroom . 
@@25370237 <p> The species , also referred to as the common Sydney octopus , or octopus tetricus -- but mostly known as the gloomy octopus because of its downcast appearance -- has been riding the East Australian Current south to the warming waters of Tasmania , after previously being confined to eastern Australia . <p> A new study , published in the journal Scientific Reports , suggests the species is thriving in Tasmanian waters with a healthy mix of genes from local and interstate populations . <p> Gloomy octopuses were first spotted in the mid-2000s by Tasmanian fishermen and citizen scientists through the Redmap project , which invites Australians to share sightings of uncommon species in an effort to map which ones are extending their habitats in response to ocean warming . <p> Scientists from the Institute for Marine and Antarctic Studies ( IMAS ) and the Centre for Marine Socioecology ( CMS ) at the University of Tasmania have been working with James Cook University to examine the changing genetics of the species . <p> Dr Jorge Ramos , who led the study as part of his PhD research at IMAS , said the octopus provided a " good @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ species to establish and persist in recently colonised areas due to oceanic warming " . <p> Dr Gretta Pecl from the CMS said the species was among thousands spreading its populations all over the world as a result of climate change . <p> " And we really do n't have a lot of information about what that means for the genetic composition of these populations . <p> " We were looking at the octopus partly to get an indication of whether it had lived here before . <p> " But also to see was there any evidence of things like a really big genetic bottleneck , or a really reduced level of genetic diversity in the population . " <p> Associate Professor Jan Strugnell from James Cook University said the research did uncover some reduced diversity in southern populations . <p> " Surprisingly , even though genetic bottlenecks were detected , genetic diversity was moderate and comparable between populations from their usual home and the new place , " she said . <h> Octopus growing ' happily , healthily ' <p> Professor Pecl said there was evidence more animals were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " Individuals from Tasmania are breeding and are supplying more animals into that area , but Tasmania 's also getting animals from Victoria and New South Wales , and we can tell that because the animals from Victoria and New South Wales have a different genetic makeup , " she said . <p> " Even though these animals are living right on the edge of their distribution and right on the edge of their limits for what they prefer in terms of environment , they 're still growing and reproducing quite happily and healthily . " <p> It is unclear how many gloomy octopuses are now living in Tasmania , but they have been found all along the state 's east coast and as far south as Hobart . <p> " We do know that it 's in high enough numbers for it to be caught commercially as part of an octopus fishery on the north coast of Tasmania , " Professor Pecl said . <h> Features <p> Since the Opposition Leader 's said an ALP government would repeal the Government 's company tax cuts , relations with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as an alternative Labor Prime Minister .

@@25373169 <h> Is Trump the end of the line for American exceptionalism ? <p> Only a war or a major terrorist attack on American soil would rouse once more the deep exceptionalist impulse in America 's view of itself and the world . Andrew Harrer <p> by James Curran <p> Since Donald Trump 's election in November 2016 the most common critique of his foreign policy is that it swings a wrecking ball through much of the liberal international order that has been the basis for prosperity and stability across much of the Western world for the past 70 years . <p> On the back of Trump 's withdrawal of the US from the Iran nuclear deal , the Trans-Pacific Partnership and the Paris climate change accords , along with his attacks on the United Nations and other multilateral institutions , the President is perceived by many as posing a direct threat to the system of global governance established by the United States in the wake of the Second World War . <p> This criticism of Trump often conceals a more serious charge : that by undermining the liberal international order he is actually diluting the power of the American @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ self-image and role in the world . <p> In his inaugural address in January 2001 , George W Bush recounted the familiar tale of America as a " new world " that had regenerated and liberated the old world . MICK TSIKAS <p> Even worse is the suggestion that he is hastening the relative decline of the United States as a global power . <p> Advertisement <h> Related Quotes <p> Unlike the majority of US presidents in the post-war period , Trump does not use the language of Pax Americana , the long-held idea that the United States is the keeper of global peace . This adds to the prevailing sense of unease among many in America and abroad . <p> In his acceptance speech as the Republican nominee , Trump proclaimed that " Americanism , not globalism , will be our credo " . In the second year of his presidency , that sentiment is hardening . That he regularly attacks the institutions and traditions of American democracy itself also challenges the very idea that the United States is a model for other societies to follow . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ American national psyche has prompted an outpouring of attempts to diagnose and remedy this malaise . <p> It is generally ascribed to Donald Trump 's election . <p> David Rowe <p> However , that judgment ignores other factors such as the damage done to the liberal international order by the US-led invasion of Iraq in 2003 . And even at the height of American power in the twentieth century , the US was unable to dictate to history . <p> As Harvard 's Joseph Nye points out , Washington could not prevent the ' loss ' of China , stalemate in Korea , the partition of Germany , Soviet suppression of insurrections within its own bloc , the survival of a communist regime in Cuba and strategic failure in Vietnam . <p> To date , Trump appears to reject the notion of a higher calling for the United States in international affairs . His administration 's National Security Strategy states that " the American way of life can not be imposed upon others , nor is it the inevitable culmination of progress " . <p> This America First approach has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by the late Charles Krauthammer as " opting for insularity and smallness " instead of global leadership , and dismissed by Bill Kristol as " profoundly depressing and vulgar " . <p> Obama 's much maligned strategy of ' leading from behind ' , along with his belief that the United States can not fix every problem in the world , was an argument for retrenchment in an attempt to fashion a more sustainable American foreign policy . AP <p> For others , the America First approach strikes at the heart of the country 's global mission . Forces once seen as mutually reinforcing are portrayed by Trump to be inherently antagonistic . <p> Robert Zoellick , a former deputy secretary of state and later World Bank president , has argued that in foreign policy Trump 's " pitting of American nationalism against the country 's internationalism " is driving a stake through the United States ' very essence . " Most often " , Zoellick added , " US nationalism and internationalism have been in synchrony ... and the mixture created America 's unique global leadership " . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , sees the task now as figuring out " how to convince people that principled nationalism and internationalism are not incompatible " . This perceived fracturing of the American ethos is disturbing to an elite nurtured by long-held beliefs in US primacy . <p> Still , the domestic grievances that brought Trump to power do suggest a longer-term shift in how America views and performs in the world . <p> When Donald Trump spoke of a " glorious destiny " for his country , he was talking about his desire to revitalise the domestic economy . Damon Winter <p> These forces -- antagonism towards globalisation , antipathy to Congress , and resentment at the blood and treasure expended on attempts at democratic transformations in Iraq and Afghanistan -- have tarnished that tradition of exceptionalism which embodies a more ambitious US global posture . <p> Indeed , a widespread view among foreign policy commentators in the United States is that the American political elite has lost the ability to sell a global foreign policy to the average voter . <p> All of this points to something of a crisis in American @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the country understands its role overseas . <p> Close allies such as Australia should place a much higher priority on understanding the nature of this ideological crisis . Instead , the more hawkish in Canberra 's national security community recite Cold War nostrums and wallow in dewy-eyed sentimentality about American leadership . <p> The current president has tapped into real and visceral feelings about the United States and its historic mission . Al Drago <h> A different kind of nationalism <p> Indeed what makes Trump so different is that the rhetoric of American exceptionalism has been a staple of US presidential speeches , with some rare exceptions , since the late nineteenth century . This approach to international affairs derives from Americans ' unique belief about themselves , their ' exceptionalism ' . <p> In contrast to that of Europeans , the American people 's nationalism is not based on ideas of race or culture . <p> Rather it is ideological . Their political leaders , historians , and culture makers spoke of a world drama that centred on a people who had fled the corruption and tyranny of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ first colonies , had set out to create a new order based on natural rights , in particular liberty and democracy . <p> These ideas had informed the revolution against an oppressive King George III and his corrupt government and were the guiding spirit behind the Declaration of Independence and the creation of the Constitution of the United States . <p> The new world , freed from the chains of European imperialism , was at last able to pursue its destiny to create a new order . <p> Set apart from the contamination of the old world , from its wars , autocratic rulers and oppression , the Americans would build a model society and become a beacon for all other peoples . <p> Perhaps not surprisingly , then , the Trump presidency has generated yet another wave of gloomy prognostications about American decline . Andrew Harrer <p> When , in the twentieth century , the United States was drawn into European and then global conflicts , Americans could only justify their military intervention as a crusade to bring democracy and liberty to these lands . Given this view of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> These were struggles between liberty and tyranny , darkness and light , good and evil . Thus the United States went to war against Spain in 1898 to fight imperialism , to free the Cubans from the tyranny of their European masters . Likewise , they entered the First World War , as President Woodrow Wilson put it , to make the world " safe for democracy " . <p> No other country proclaimed such a motive . As historians have pointed out , the Second World War was a crusade against Fascism , the Cold War against Communism , and the war against Afghanistan and Iraq a crusade against terrorism . <p> US presidents have often drawn on this idea of American exceptionalism to illuminate their times and to justify the US role in the world . <p> At a high point in the Cold War , President John F Kennedy spoke of America 's confrontation of the Soviet Union in such terms . In his inaugural address in January 1961 he reminded Americans that as " heirs of that first revolution " , its ideals were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " to pay any price , bear any burden ... to assure the survival and success of liberty " . <p> Despite failure in Vietnam , this idea was given renewed life in the 1980s under the leadership of Ronald Reagan . In his first Fourth of July message , Reagan hailed the American Revolution as " the only true philosophical revolution in all history " . <p> He dismissed earlier revolutions because they had " simply exchanged one set of rulers for another " . In Reagan 's eyes , what happened in 1776 was unique : it was the first revolution to change the " very concept of government " and to establish government on the basis of human rights . These principles gave Americans a moral position in the world that set them apart from and above all other peoples . <p> At the end of the Cold War , President George HW Bush announced that " by the grace of God " America had emerged victorious from the struggle against Communism . Its providential mission had been vindicated . A world " once divided into two @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one sole and pre-eminent power " . Bush reminded his fellow Americans that they were not only the freest and kindest nation on earth but now also the strongest . They would continue to be " a rising nation , the once and future miracle that is still ... the hope of the world " . <p> David Rowe <p> The sentiments that characterised the ' American century ' were carried over into the new one . In his inaugural address in January 2001 , George W Bush recounted the familiar tale of America as a " new world " that had regenerated and liberated the old world . <p> American faith in liberty and democracy had stood up and survived many great trials . Its mission continued because " America 's faith in freedom and democracy was a rock in a raging sea . Now it is a seed upon the wind , taking root in many nations . " <p> Even when trying to justify America 's role in Iraq after two years of war , Bush refused to be downhearted and invoked the ideal that freedom would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " founders declared a new order of the ages , when soldiers died in wave upon wave for a union based on liberty , when citizens marched under the banner ' Freedom Now ' , they were acting on an ancient hope that is meant to be fulfilled " . <p> Like the Liberty Bell in Philadelphia , Bush pronounced that America " proclaims liberty throughout the world and to all the inhabitants thereof " . <h> Doubt and disillusion in the American mission <p> But Trump is not the first president in the past half century to raise doubts about America 's historic mission , nor the first to preside over a period of doubt and disillusion about the nation 's role in the world . That debate has been surfacing periodically since at least the time of the Vietnam War . <p> The answer of the Nixon administration at that time was to abandon the worldwide struggle against Communism , reach an accommodation with Communist China , and pursue d ? tente with the Soviet Union . <p> Two years earlier , Nixon had also set out what @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ New York Times <p> " When we see the world in which we are about to move " , said Nixon in a speech delivered in mid-1971 , " the United States no longer is in the position of complete pre-eminence or predominance . That is not a bad thing . " <p> Two years earlier , Nixon had also set out what would later come to be called the Nixon doctrine : in essence , calling for US allies in the region to provide more for their own self defence , and signalling that Washington would not again involve itself in another land war in Asia . <p> As the Cold War ended , new questions were raised about America 's identity and role in the world . <p> Historian Michael Vlahos observed in 1990 -- in words that could easily apply now -- that " our foreign policy musings today are shot through with domestic melancholy " , adding that those debating inside the Beltway were " out of touch with the people they serve " . Vlahos believed the American idea to be " at risk , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The period from the end of the Cold War to the terrorist attacks on September 11 , 2001 was defined by a constant search for a coherent foreign policy doctrine . Many elites feared that in the absence of the Soviet threat it would be harder to rouse Americans to the call of global leadership . <p> The quest for meaning also coloured the presidencies of Bill Clinton , George W Bush , and Barack Obama . For advocates of the Pax Americana , Secretary of State Madeleine Albright 's 1998 description of the United States as the " indispensable nation " seemed fitting in terms of defining the nation 's post-Cold War global role , and became intertwined with NATO enlargement and the rationale for intervention in the Balkans . <p> The term also blended with the outlook and intent of the neoconservative Project for a New American Century -- both were adamant that the United States had to continue shouldering the burden of global leadership . <p> Even though George W Bush commenced his presidency by arguing that the United States could not continue to play @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of 9/11 he gave full voice to the policy and practice of aggressive and unilateral democracy promotion . <p> The quest for meaning also coloured the presidencies of Bill Clinton , George W Bush , and Barack Obama . Reuters <p> American values were expressed primarily through military power . <p> Obama 's much maligned strategy of ' leading from behind ' , along with his belief that the United States can not fix every problem in the world , was an argument for retrenchment in an attempt to fashion a more sustainable American foreign policy . In the wake of the failed attempt to remake the Middle East , this was a tactical retreat from what he saw as the overextension of his neoconservative predecessor . <p> Obama also began his presidency by questioning the exceptionalist tradition . <p> When asked by a journalist in early 2009 about America 's special standing in the world , he replied : " I believe in American exceptionalism , just as I suspect that the Brits believe in British exceptionalism and the Greeks believe in Greek exceptionalism . " <p> To critics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ American exceptionalism on a par with that of other countries , not above them . This unleashed a torrent of criticism . <p> Former Republican presidential candidate Mike Huckabee argued that " to deny American exceptionalism is , in essence , to deny the heart and soul of this nation " . <p> Obama then spent much of the remainder of his presidency using the rhetoric of exceptionalism -- trying , in effect , to be more exceptional than the exceptionalists . <p> If few presidents have challenged the exceptionalist orthodoxy , fewer still have done so in an inaugural address . <p> When Donald Trump , on the steps of the Capitol in January 2017 , spoke of a " glorious destiny " for his country , he was not talking about America as a chosen nation about to set forth on another worldwide crusade , but about his desire to revitalise the domestic economy , to deliver for the " forgotten men and women " who swept him to power . <p> Similarly , his idea of an America " winning again " was a clarion call to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ war , and it was most certainly not a cry for the export of American liberty and democracy . <p> Perhaps not surprisingly , then , the Trump presidency has generated yet another wave of gloomy prognostications about American decline . A New York Times editorial in October 2017 concluded that the damage Trump had inflicted on the liberal international order was tantamount to surrendering US leadership to Russia and China , countries from which he had promised to protect Americans . <p> The comparison between the chaos and dysfunction of Trump 's White House and the tightening power grip of Chinese President Xi Jinping provokes claims that China will soon remove the United States from its hegemonic perch . <p> It is possible , however , that Trump is nothing more than an interloper in the saga of American progress , albeit a disruptive one . <p> On this interpretation , once Trump has left the scene normal programming will resume , and the United States will once more assume its leadership mantle . <p> Those who believe this console themselves that the naysayers about the United States have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ following the Vietnam War and Watergate -- and so will be again . America will renew and replenish , just as it has in the past . <p> This may , of course , be right : the US economy recorded a 2.9 per cent growth rate in the last three months of 2017 , and with a bullish stock market , rising business confidence , low-cost energy in abundance and a new wave of digital innovation rising , the American dream could well be rebuilt . <p> This theme of American resilience is a refuge from the Trumpian storm for the foreign policy elites rusted on to their interpretation of American exceptionalism . <p> However , it is also predicated on the assumption that the United States does not have to relinquish its hegemonic hubris . <h> Exceptional normalisation <p> What underscores the significance of the lack of idealism in Trump 's own vision is that in the past the United States has paid high economic and human costs for the commitment to its national ideals . <p> For a quarter of a century after the Communists came to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , regarding its rise as a betrayal of the Chinese people who had thereby become enslaved to Moscow . <p> The Americans treated the People 's Republic of China as a pariah nation and banned Americans from any contact or connection with the country , including economic ties such as trade and investment . <p> Since their allies , including Australia , would not agree to such extreme measures and continued to trade profitably with Beijing , the United States paid a high price for being true to what its national ideals dictated . <p> Likewise , the United States suffered great loss of lives and treasure in the Vietnam War , which it fought in the name of freedom to keep the Communist bloc at bay . There was no material interest that could have justified paying such a price . <p> The United States now has a president , however , who is not given to proselytising that version of the American mission . For all of Trump 's clarion calls about American " greatness " , the United States will have to accept the disconnect between its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transformational change abroad . <p> This period , then , may well come to be seen as the first step in preparing Americans for the end of global hegemony . <p> Walter Russell Mead contends that Trump 's coming may not be as " ill-suited to the country 's needs as his most fervid detractors believe " , primarily because he is bringing to the fore the harsh reality that the country 's post-Cold War national security strategy has " run out of gas " . <p> Seen in this light , Trump is the president America had to have : a leader immune to the siren song of grandiose globalism , a commander-in-chief who appears to grasp that the United States can no longer succumb to the dangers of imperial arrogance . <p> However , a quick reality check is needed here . <p> America will not become a ' normal nation ' . It will not lose entirely that sense of special mission , simply because that part of its national creed runs so deep and because those in the foreign policy elite are creatures of their culture @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was the America of Kennedy , Johnson , Reagan , and Clinton . <p> The Washington elite will not easily abandon their taste for being a superpower . <p> They will cling tightly to the vision and vigour inherent in the description of the United States as the " indispensable nation " . <p> Any major breakthrough on the question of North Korean denuclearisation , for example , would likely be framed by some in the administration in such terms , even though Trump himself argues that progress on negotiations with Pyongyang emanates from his special deal-making skills rather than any innate sense of American benevolence or leadership . Talk by national security adviser John Bolton of regime change in Iran also shows the resilience of these ideas . <p> And in a recent ' pep talk ' to US diplomats , Secretary of State Mike Pompeo spoke of his desire to help the State Department regain its " swagger " , a term he went on to say was based on " America 's essential rightness ... an aggressiveness born of the righteous knowledge that our cause is just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " . <p> Middle America , however , has lost faith in the US global mission , and the country may very well be at the beginning of a long period of what Financial Times columnist Ed Luce calls " exceptional normalisation " . <p> If this is right , the ungluing of this core national belief will be a painful process . Those who argue for American renewal , as historian Neville Meaney argued , typically " find it hard to contemplate decline " since " to accept such a future would mean a rejection of all that lies at the heart of their ... national identity " . <p> Compounding the problem is China 's rise , which presents the idea of American exceptionalism with an altogether different challenge . It is difficult to see a US president or national security adviser developing a strategy to accommodate Chinese power -- even though the United States may ultimately have to do so , if grudgingly . Still , those who predicted US decline in the 1970s witnessed the rebirth of American purpose under Reagan and the subsequent collapse of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about to collapse , it does face substantial demographic , environmental and social challenges in the years ahead , challenges that might work to America 's long-term advantage . <p> Since 1945 a key assumption about America 's foreign policy is that it is inherently internationalist . This has certainly been the case for much of the past 70 years . However , there is another tradition of American international behaviour that is selfish , inward-looking , and nationalistic . It was that tradition which emerged predominant in 2016 . Trump rode those sentiments , injected them with a new intensity and became their ultimate beneficiary . <p> Trumpism , therefore , is no fleeting phenomenon -- the current president has tapped into real and visceral feelings about the United States and its historic mission . <p> Only a war or a major terrorist attack on American soil would rouse once more the deep exceptionalist impulse in America 's view of itself and the world . Absent such a crisis , however , there is likely to be more damage to US prestige and credibility under Trump , making recovery @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Affairs editor Gideon Rose , Trump " has complicated significantly the job of the next president in restoring and updating the liberal international order " , adding that " the job will be harder because many abroad , especially US allies , will be wondering if the US is even committed to it " . <p> Close allies will need to think much differently , therefore , about American staying power . They will need to be more finely attuned to the anger and frustration pouring out of middle America , along with the repercussions they have on Washington 's self-confidence and capacity . <p> James Curran is a Non-Resident Fellow at the Lowy Institute and Professor of Modern History at Sydney University . This article is extracted from his forthcoming Lowy Analysis Paper , Americanism , not Globalism ' : President Trump and the American Mission , to be released on 3 July . 
@@25377836 <h> Tropical Forest Loss Slowed in 2017 -- To the Second Worst Total Ever <p> IMAGINE LOOKING DOWN on a huge swath of lush forest -- but before you can pull out your phone and take a picture , it 's gone . <p> In tropical regions around the world , tree cover is disappearing that quickly : Every minute of every day over the last two years , a tract the size of 40 football fields was clear-cut or burned to increase production of soy , cattle , palm oil , and wood products . <p> Despite efforts to reduce tropical deforestation , tree cover loss has nearly doubled over the past 15 years . In 2017 , 39 million acres ( 15.8 million hectares ) disappeared -- an area close to the size of Washington State -- according to new data released Wednesday by the research group World Resources Institute ( WRI ) at the Oslo Tropical Forest Forum , where 500 forest experts and policymakers are meeting about the issue . The latest total was second only to 2016 , the worst-ever year of tropical forest loss with 41.7 million acres ( 16.9 million hectares ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also playing an increasing role in forest loss , especially as climate change makes them more frequent and severe , according to the report . The regions that lost the most forest in 2017 were in Latin America , Southeast Asia , and Central Africa . <p> While destroying forests releases huge volumes of carbon dioxide , growing forests capture it from the atmosphere , making forest protection one of the keys to limiting climate change . And since tropical forests grow year-round , they are especially important . Proper conservation and restoration of tropical forests , mangroves , and peatlands could provide a cost-effective way to achieve up to 23 percent of the carbon dioxide reductions needed by 2030 , according a WRI working paper on climate and tropical forests released at the Oslo forum . <p> Despite this , countries and the private sector spend about $100 billion a year subsidizing and investing in forest-destroying agricultural expansion and land development , said Frances Seymour , distinguished senior fellow at WRI . Meanwhile , she adds , only a billion dollars a year goes toward forest conservation . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fire with a teaspoon while more gas is being poured on the flame , " she said . <h> Ranching Is the Biggest Cause <p> A lot of that $100 billion a year in subsidies and investments goes toward exports of food and wood products to other countries . China and India are among the world 's biggest importers of soy , pulp and paper , and palm oil . China alone is a huge importer of beef , and cattle ranching is the largest single cause of deforestation , according to a WRI working paper on deforestation-free supply chains . <p> These subsidies and investments should be redirected to increasing sustainable agriculture on non-forest lands , said Andreas Dahl-J ? rgensen , deputy director of Norway 's International Climate and Forest Initiative during a press conference about the new data . <p> The rate of tree cover loss is less than half in community and indigenous lands compared to elsewhere . However , that comes at a high price . The human rights group Global Witness documented 197 murders of people defending land and environmental rights in 2017 . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a UN expert and indigenous leader . Recognizing and supporting legal rights of the world 's indigenous people -- who occupy more than 50 percent of the world 's land -- is a powerful tool for protecting forests and the climate , she said . <h> Five Takeaways from the New Report <p> * Brazil is still by far the deforestation leader with 11.1 million acres ( 4.5 million hectares ) lost in 2017 , followed by the Democratic Republic of Congo with 3.7 million acres ( 1.5 million hectares ) . <p> * Colombia 's deforestation was three times higher in 2017 than 2015 -- the end of the civil war has resulted in a land rush for cattle ranching , mining , soy , timber , and land speculation . <p> * The Congo halted industrial logging 16 years ago , but forest loss increased in 2016-17 . @@25411414 <p> Almost $13 billion has been set aside to rescue the Murray-Darling river system from environmental disaster but what is being achieved with that money ? <p> Farmers use more than 80 per cent of the water consumed in the basin to produce a third of the nation 's food ( ABS figures ) . <p> According to the Murray-Darling Basin Authority , the basin is home to more than two million people and covers about 14 per cent of Australia 's landmass . <p> The authority says the basin includes a range of diverse landscapes and complex ecosystems , including over 77,000 km of rivers and more than 25,000 wetlands . <p> These landscapes are home to at least 35 endangered species of birds , 16 endangered species of mammals and 46 known species of native fish . <p> The purpose of the Murray-Darling Basin Plan is to return water to the river and to balance the needs of communities with the needs of the environment that is home to unique species of plants , fish , birds and animals . <p> More than $8 billion of the $13 billion has been committed specifically for irrigation infrastructure and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ far ? <h> Chowilla Floodplain : Rare birds coming back <p> The regent parrot nests in tree hollows and much of its habitat has been destroyed , but they are recovering in some areas where environmental watering is bringing the trees back to life . <p> The regent parrot nests in tree hollows and much of its habitat has been destroyed , but they are recovering in some areas where environmental watering is bringing the trees back to life . <p> Supplied : Mallee Catchment Management Authority <p> The Chowilla Floodplain is recognised internationally as an important site , but unique species like the regent parrot and the magnificent forests of river red gums are being threatened by drought and a lack of water . <p> Kevin Smith has spent 30 years monitoring bird life on the Murray and has come to appreciated the value of big infrastructure projects to manage the environment . <p> He has dedicated the past 30 years to surveying bird life on the Chowilla Floodplain and he has seen the impact of water loss on the trees and bird life . <p> " Even those @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the regent parrots were declining along with them . " <p> Mr Smith was originally opposed to the regulator but he has seen the difference the man made structures can make . <p> " We will get the whole food web , the whole cycle of life going ... and everything will start breeding from the very microscopic up to the water birds , " Mr Smith said . <p> So far $5 billion has been spent on infrastructure and about $3 billion on buying water for the environment . <p> The Government intends to continue prioritising infrastructure projects over purchasing water and that is angering environmental groups who say that the strategy is flawed by dodgy accounting and will result is less water flowing down the river . <p> The Chowilla regulator controls environmental flows into the floodplain . <p> Black Box trees have prospered and some rare birds are also making a comeback . <p> Professor Richard Loyn from Latrobe University who is a regular visitor to Hattah Lakes , said this spring there were sightings of an Australian painted snipe , listed as critically endangered , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ officer at the Department of Primary Industries , said environmental flows could also provide an opportunity for extinct local fish species to be reintroduced . <p> He said this included the rare ones like the purple spotted gudgeon , southern pygmy perch , Macquarie perch and trout cod . <p> " Things that used to be fairly common in this part of the world but have disappeared , " Mr Ellis said . <p> Rob Mansell , an irrigator , lives on the edge of Hattah Lakes . His water turned black after an environmental water flow . <p> Anna Parker , a project manager with the North Central Catchment Management Authority , who was involved in the clean up , said the authority did some netting and electro fishing to remove all the large bodied carp from the wetland . <p> " So when we delivered the environmental water we gave the vegetation the best chance to germinate and set seed , " Ms Parker said . <p> It was a big success , she said , with a large area of swamp wallaby grass germinating naturally , providing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have been really great , the water birds have come in and all of the aquatic vegetation you see in the water is a response to low carp numbers . " <p> Only a small portion of the forest can be artificially watered however and the rest will deteriorate over time . <p> " That area will become more territorialised because of the rate of change and climate change and its going through that quite rapidly . " <h> Northern Basin : New science , different approach <p> The Balonne River at St George in southern Queensland . The northern basin is very different from the south and the focus has been on supplementing flows to ephemeral water holes on rivers running across the NSW border from Queensland into the Darling . <p> The Balonne River at St George in southern Queensland . The northern basin is very different from the south and the focus has been on supplementing flows to ephemeral water holes on rivers running across the NSW border from Queensland into the Darling . <p> ABC : Arlie Felton-Taylor <p> The science behind much of this work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as the effort to re-stock the Murray cod . <p> It is Australia 's largest freshwater fish and one of the most popular recreational fish in this country . <p> Commercial fishing in the first half of the 20th century brought the species to the edge of extinction , so a community group on the Queensland/New South Wales border has been releasing Murray Cod fingerlings to help bring them back . <p> Brian Dare is from a fish restocking group on the Southern Downs in Queensland that has been releasing Murray cod fingerlings to help rejuvenate the species . <h> What is the Murray-Darling Basin Plan and why are we still talking about it ? <p> The river systems are much more ephemeral , they have much bigger wetting and drying cycle , and water authorities are still learning how to manage the environment by mimicking nature . <p> For example , releasing environmental water from dams has badly affected some fish species . <p> According to NSW senior inland fisheries manager Cameron Westaway , releasing cold water at the wrong time can play havoc with fish breeding . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are using temperature as a trigger to go into spawning conditions and ... that cold water can skew their whole breeding cycle . " <p> The cold water affects other parts of the eco-system as well . <p> " The tiny little crustaceans , the algae the plants , everything like that are affected ... and the fish can taste the difference . " <p> For many scientists working on the Murray-Darling Basin plan , it is trial by error . <h> Early days , but progress is being made <p> There are many critics of the Basin Plan . <p> Irrigators and the communities that rely on the river system say the water buybacks are killing their economies and their towns . <p> Environmentalists say not enough water is being returned to the system to ensure it will survive and the plan is failing . <p> The Murray-Darling Basin Authority said it was still early days in the life of the plan but there were some signs of success . <p> " Although we are still early on in a long-term water reform process , the evidence shows that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there have been positive changes , " a spokesman said . <p> Just four per cent of the flow down the Murray-Darling Basin makes it to the Coorong in South Australia and some environmentalists suggest it is now so degraded it should be taken off the RAMSAR list of internationally significant wetlands . <p> Just four per cent of the flow down the Murray-Darling Basin makes it to the Coorong in South Australia and some environmentalists suggest it is now so degraded it should be taken off the RAMSAR list of internationally significant wetlands .
